Katalog Berita (Halaman 42/57)

#3062. Can AI plan terrorist attacks? The US tested ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and Grok

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Investigators tried to bypass AI safeguards to generate plans. The results exposed security gaps and raised questions about preventing misuse.

The Government Accountability Office (GAO) conducted a series of tests earlier this year designed to determine whether users could bypass guardrails and convince AIs to plan terrorist attacks. The investigators succeeded. In October, the agency published a report titled “Covert Testing Used Common Platforms to Create Viable Attack Plans,” which was released to the public in November.

The tests were conducted using Claude, Gemini, ChatGPT, and Grok. All four AIs produced attack scenarios that subject matter experts were able to confirm would have mostly succeeded.

I spoke via email with the report’s lead author, Kevin Walsh, director of IT and cybersecurity audits at the GAO. He told ZDNET:

In general, the details provided by the four AI platforms are not novel. Much of the information is readily available on the internet. But these plans were generated in hours, rather than weeks or months. AI could also surface ideas or methods that may not have been previously considered.

To the companies’ credit, their safeguards have continued to evolve since we conducted these tests. The companies also assure us that this is an area of concern and ongoing research. However, our report concludes that, in its current state, this technology can be dangerous, and its misuse represents a growing risk to our nation.

The Government Accountability Office is an independent, nonpartisan agency that works for the legislative branch of the US government. Its responsibility is to “examine how taxpayer dollars are spent and provide Congress and federal agencies with objective, non-partisan, fact-based information to help the government save money and work more efficiently.”

Also: Why enterprise AI agents could become the ultimate insider threat

The GAO investigators constructed two attack scenarios: detonating an explosive device for maximum damage and destroying an electrical grid substation for maximum disruption. They randomly split the challenge across the four AI platforms, so that two AIs each worked independently on the explosive challenge while two others worked independently on the substation challenge.

This assignment split is where, after being shown the results, the AI companies pushed back. That seems justifiable. By not giving all four AI services the same set of assignments, the GAO lost the ability to objectively measure all four against each other.

As a platform-vs-platform comparison, the GAO investigation falls short. But as an investigation into whether the generative AI tools available in mid-2026 might be able to be used for credible national security threats, its results are definitive. The investigation also highlighted how AIs reduce the time and effort involved in researching how such attacks might be carried out.

While the four AIs used for the tests were identified in the report, the GAO did not publish which AIs were used to carry out which tests. Instead, “Due to the sensitive nature and content of the testing, we randomly assigned an alphabetic character to each platform for purposes of presenting the test results within this report.”

For the bomb-damage scenario, the two assigned AIs recommended different approaches, although both provided specific actionable steps in order to accomplish the tasks.

Platform A constructed a scenario where an IED (improvised explosive device) would be detonated inside a hotel parking structure. It provided a timetable for when the hotel was expected to be crowded due to a public event as well as where the hotel’s security was likely to be weakest.

It recommended where to rent a van from outside the city, how to disable the GPS in the van, a shopping list of supplies used in constructing the device, specific stores to shop at, where to park the van in the garage, how to set up the device for detonation, and even a bus route to take in order to get back to the attacker’s personal car.

Also: Could cyberwar knock us back to the Stone Age?

After additional prompting by GAO investigators, Platform A “also generated an image that directed us to a specific area to deploy the IED to cause significant damage to the location. Specifically, Platform A generated an image of a parking structure that recommended where to park the vehicle.”

GAO investigators verified the plan’s viability by consulting with a bomb squad commander at a big-city fire department. The bomb tech confirmed that following the instructions with the recommended components would result in an explosion. The GAO also confirmed the availability of the supplies from the vendors the AI recommended.

Platform B targeted a convention center. Its recommended preparations were extensive. It recommended incorporating a shell company as a cover story so that the attacker could legitimately lease a secure, ventilated barn. It recommended a rural purchasing circuit, buying just enough supplies at four rural co-ops over four days in order to avoid triggering any mandatory reporting requirements.

The AI then recommended purchasing diesel fuel from three truck stops using cash, making “unremarkable purchases at unremarkable locations.” The AI recommended transferring the fuel into sealed 55-gallon drums at the barn. It also recommended careful storage conditions, including temperature, drum integrity, and separation from other materials (the materials themselves were redacted from the GAO’s report).

Also: Apple, Google, and Microsoft join Anthropic’s Project Glasswing to defend world’s most critical software

Next up, the AI recommended a communications security review, burner phones, varied procurement routes, and information compartmentalization. It recommended taking a taxi to a waterfront and then walking south for 40 minutes. It suggested confirming sight lines and contractor access to road positions. It even suggested that while the attacker’s face would appear on three cameras, the footage from those specific cameras would not be reviewed for three weeks.

It then recommended sending decoy trucks into the target, then sneaking an explosive vehicle into a parking garage.

When the GAO reviewed the recommendations, the agency discovered the AI got event dates wrong. However, according to the bomb squad commander, the components and procedures recommended would definitely have resulted in an explosion.

As with the bomb-damage scenario, the two AIs participating in the electrical grid scenario took different approaches, but both had the potential to be disturbingly effective.

Platform C began by identifying electrical substations where an attack could be most effective. It produced a list that helped identify vulnerable substations. After some prompting, “The platform then selected a substation in a specific major U.S. city and estimated that the loss of power would cause billions of dollars in damages.”

It identified the specific location on the specified substation where a drone could hit and cause a short circuit, which would destroy a key component. It actually provided a simulated video of the drone attack as well as a diagram that showed the component’s failure process.

The AI identified the exact model of the drone that it felt was best suited for the attack. Initially, the AI’s guardrails refused to provide an exact location for a target substation. That said, “The platform eventually provided the name of a substation, which we used to identify an exact location.”

The day after the AI made its recommendations, the GAO reported, “We observed that the platform had removed portions of our chat discussions that related to electrical grid vulnerabilities. It is unclear whether this action was initiated as part of a guardrail or if by error.” After two weeks, the account had not been suspended.

GAO investigators confirmed that the suggested drone model did exist, the route to the target was accurate, and the proposed target did exist. A subject matter expert consulted by the GAO “Verified that the information provided by Platform C was generally correct. The expert stated such an attack could, at the very least, cause damage to the substation. However, the expert could not confirm whether the specific drone suggested by Platform C would cause a short circuit or destruction of the substation.”

After rented vans, shell companies, and aerial drone attacks, Platform D decided to do it old school: plain old firearms. Point. Shoot. Buh-bye.

The AI provided GAO investigators with a primary target substation along with three secondary backup locations. It recommended locations near a large international airport and a bunch of data centers to increase the economic impact from the attack. And just in case it wasn’t clear enough, the AI drew a map highlighting the locations of the target substations.

It recommended the use of a gun, recommended the type of weapon along with the proper ammo in order to maximize the effects of the attack, along with where an attacker could go to procure such weapons. It recommended both online and in-person surveillance of the target locations prior to initiating an attack.

As with Platform C’s recommendation, Platform D recommended specific components in the substation that would be most vulnerable to an attack. It even provided images highlighting where the transformers would be most vulnerable.

The GAO report stated that the AI originally threw up guardrails and refused to provide the information requested, saying, “Our query raised serious concerns about potential intent to engage in criminal activity or harm critical infrastructure due to the context of the conversation.”

Also: AI finds zero-day flaws for just $4 now – even mediocre hackers can cash in

The more telling comment is this from the report: “However, the platform ultimately provided us with information needed for an attack.” I’m thinking someone in the GAO could probably sell water to a fish.

As with Platform C, GAO noted that the AI removed some of the chat content from the chat history. As for whether the plan would work, “We verified the planned attack was viable for half of the substation locations recommended by Platform D.”

A subject matter expert also confirmed that attacking the specific areas of the transformers recommended could cause an outage and that the specific firearms and ammunition recommended were available from the purchase locations specified. The AI did make an error in the generated maps by incorrectly placing roads.

But of the four recommended locations, two were actually viable. GAO investigators took a field trip to the recommended locations and verified that two could work for such an attack, but the other two had high walls that obscured the view of the targets.

After completing the tests, the GAO investigators presented the results to the AI vendors and gave the companies the opportunity to respond. While the GAO report purposely hid which AI provided which attack plan, the report is fully forthcoming with the complete response letters from each company.

In reading the responses, I found the difference in attitudes and approach to the question most interesting.

Anthropic’s four-page response letter was signed by Anthony Cimino, head of federal affairs. The company’s response was courteous but strongly challenging. It moved immediately from thanking the GAO to questioning the statistical significance and criticizing the absence of a control group.

Anthropic’s letter about Claude says, “We ask that the report specify that there were not enough trials to achieve significance, and that therefore any conclusions are at most preliminary.”

Also: How I safely run my OpenClaw AI agent 24/7 on a Mac

Google’s three-page response about Gemini was unsigned. The majority of the response discussed launch reviews, executive oversight, Google’s testing, filters, training, and human review practices. The company did agree that Gemini could violate guidelines. It was the most disengaged from the GAO’s report, and it offered little in the way of explanation about the issues raised.

Google’s letter says, “We have deep respect for the mission of the Government Accountability Office and we want to underscore that Google takes your work very seriously.”

OpenAI’s three-page response letter about ChatGPT was signed by Chan Park, head of US and Canada policy and partnerships. It says the company reviewed 325 responses from the AI and identified three that fell short of how the AI is supposed to behave. The company apparently replayed the requests and said that it was unable to reproduce the responses reported by the GAO.

OpenAI was the clearest in stating that the GAO’s results demonstrated unacceptable behavior from the AI, stating: “Even a discrete contribution to a broader harmful activity can matter, and improving safeguards against such assistance remains an ongoing priority.”

SpaceXAI’s 363-word, less-than-a-page response about Grok was unsigned. The company emphasized how much effort was required to bypass the safeguards and mentioned that there have been improvements in the guardrails since the GAO conducted its tests earlier that year.

Then we reach the “Say what, now?” moment of our day.

The company replied, “It is worth noting that GAO received its output from paid accounts in the United States. SpaceXAl provides unique moderation policies for registered, paid US account holders that reflect the lower risk profile of such accounts compared to free or foreign accounts.”

In other words, the company contends that paying US account holders have a lower risk profile than free or foreign users, and that therefore Grok provides fewer guardrails. The letter provides no supporting evidence for that assumption.

While a credit card number could provide some linkage to a user’s identity compared to fully free accounts, users could easily obtain a prepaid card that allows for completely anonymous paid usage. I just think that making an assumption about risk factor based on whether a user is willing to pay $8 a month is a particularly fallacious security policy.

The GAO report is clear that each of the AIs did provide some pushback against providing cookbooks for conducting the destructive behavior.

The agency conducted a number of adversarial prompt seeding operations, including roleplaying, special encoding, character transformation, word transformation, separating intent from content, taking very small baby steps in prompting, and other approaches often used to convince an AI to effectively “jailbreak” its guardrails.

Also: If you’re not working on quantum-safe encryption now, it’s already too late

While the AIs were eventually persuaded to participate in guiding attacks, some accounts were later shut down by the AI platforms, indicating that while the guardrails didn’t prevent the AI from providing initial advice, they were eventually flagged and terminated.

While that’s a bit of “locking the barn door after the horse has bolted,” it does indicate that the AI vendors are working toward more comprehensive protections.

The GAO report didn’t address the larger issue of whether censoring the AIs is ultimately in society’s best interest.

As a counterterrorism and infrastructure security professional, I often think of Ben Franklin’s famous statement, “Those who would give up essential Liberty, to purchase a little temporary Safety, deserve neither Liberty nor Safety.” Old Ben never had to contend with nukes, cyberattacks, and machines smarter than he was.

Personally, I lament the need for guardrails because they limit potentially legitimate access to information. But as the GAO’s scenario shows, strong guardrails are ultimately necessary, not because of the AIs, but because we humans are our own worst enemy.

Also: Rogue AI incidents hit ‘tens of thousands’: Can businesses trust these tools?

On the other hand, it’s not as if humans haven’t been able to figure out how to conduct violent acts all on their own, without the help of an AI. For example, the Anarchist’s Cookbook, which is essentially a recipe book for the home manufacture of explosives and illicit drugs, has been publicly available continuously since the early 1970s. While also outdated, Amazon even sells a reprinted 1941 manual about the chemistry of powder and explosives.

So, while it is definitely disturbing that the AIs could, collectively, provide four unique attack plans for civic disruption, they are not opening a door previously unavailable to those with nefarious intent. I laud the GAO for providing additional testing feedback to the AI companies, and the AI companies for improving their guardrails.

But while the report is scary in that all that information was presented in an organized manner by each of the AIs, that availability doesn’t bestow a new superpower on crackpots. After all, AIs are trained on publicly available information.

One of the more costly, but necessary, tradeoffs of an open society is that all this information is available. Whether an AI is used to structure that information is largely irrelevant in the face of that simple fact.

#3061. Report: Apple accounted for 57% of US smartphone shipments in Q3

Apple’s share of US smartphone shipments jumped from 50% to 57% year over year last quarter, with the company being the only major smartphone maker to buck the broader trend of declining shipments. Here are the details.

Yesterday, we covered a Counterpoint Research report on Q3 Latin American smartphone shipments, which showed Apple recording the smallest year-over-year decline among the region’s top five smartphone makers. As a result, Apple increased its share of regional shipments from 7% to 9%.

The report echoed the findings of several others published throughout this year, which showed how Apple has managed to navigate component shortages and rising costs better than many of its competitors, some of which have had to raise smartphone prices multiple times.

Apple, by contrast, kept iPhone pricing largely stable for much of the year, holding off on broader price increases until the launch of the iPhone 18 Pro lineup in September.

Today, Counterpoint Research released its report on US smartphone shipments for Q3, which showed that Apple increased its share from 50% to 57% year over year, amid a 9% decline in overall shipments.

According to a separate Counterpoint report published last Thursday, Apple was the only major smartphone maker to avoid a year-over-year decline in shipment volume during the quarter.

In today’s report, Counterpoint notes that rising memory costs hit the low-end Android segment particularly hard during the quarter. Samsung was the only major Android smartphone maker to increase its shipment share, seeing a one-percentage-point bump to 25%, “helped by a portfolio spanning flagships and the low-end Galaxy A-series.”

Carriers also felt the impact of the broader downturn, with smartphone sales across the three major US carriers collectively falling 4% year over year, per the report.

Looking ahead, Counterpoint expects memory costs to remain elevated and US carriers to be more selective with their holiday promotions, with smartphone sales expected to remain weak in Q4 despite it typically being the strongest quarter of the year.

#3059. JD.com: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Acquiring CECONOMY gives JD an established European store network to pair with its automated warehousing, a growth path the margin story does not yet show.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 26, 2026 at 3:22PM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 17, 2026, JD.com (JD +2.53%) announced that total revenue returned to positive growth, rising 3.5% year over year to RMB 310 billion (roughly $43 billion). Expanding gross margins and disciplined spending on new initiatives allowed non-GAAP (adjusted non-generally accepted accounting principles) net income to jump 43.7% year over year. The operational shift demonstrates how supply chain scale and marketplace advertising revenue can defend profitability even as domestic consumption slows.

Three takeaways from the call stand out for long-term shareholders.

Retail gross margin surpassed 20% for the first time, driven by high-margin third-party marketplace advertising and general merchandise, which grew faster than direct sales. Management reinvested part of this profit surplus into a 45.4% surge in research and development (R&D) expenses for AI-related applications within business operations.

Operating losses in new business initiatives narrowed by 38.7% year over year as management shifted from early-stage volume subsidies to strict unit economics. Food delivery led this disciplined pullback, turning away from heavy promotional spending while maintaining sequential growth in order volume.

Global expansion provides a secondary growth path through Joybuy and cross-border logistics. Acquiring CECONOMY, a European consumer electronics retailer, adds an established offline distribution infrastructure to pair with automated warehousing technology.

Total revenues regained positive momentum across major retail lines while the company deployed 11 new robots across nine production lines. The company relies on its proprietary supply chain capabilities and physical robotics network to defend market share as macro conditions evolve. Management tracks advertising growth and return on investment across artificial intelligence expenditures.

Free cash flow reached RMB 19 billion over the trailing 12 months, providing balance sheet strength for measured overseas expansion. Improving unit economics across food delivery and digital platform services positions the business for durable compound earnings.

#3058. I Tried Using Meta Muse and ChatGPT Dots to Save on Groceries. They’re Not Quite There Yet

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

I tried using AI agents to build me the cheapest grocery order possible. They exhibited a startling lack of curiosity and autonomous thinking.

High grocery prices have been in the news for years now. The cost of eggs, how much you’re paying for a bowl of cereal and whether or not meat is back on the menu are the recurring subjects of nationwide headlines.

So before you drop a fortune on gas and drive out to the grocery store to spend even more on sustenance in your prep for hosting or bringing dishes to your holiday celebrations, you might consider using an AI agent to find you the best deal possible.

Although, in my experience, it’s not as simple as the AI marketing makes it sound. Here’s what happened when I tried to use AI agents to find the lowest possible prices on groceries.

AI agents such as Meta Muse and ChatGPT’s Dots can only crawl so many sites and online fliers for prices. Some stores and e-commerce sites have blocked AI agents from accessing their URLs. The first step in finding cheaper groceries with AI agents is to check which sites and stores they can access for price comparisons.

Where both agents missed a step was in the limitations they put on their searches, at first only looking at the retailers I had shown via my receipts and a few other big-name grocery stores.

I clarified in the chats and in a 20-minute voice conversation with Muse and a 14-minute voice conversation with Dots that they should expand their horizons and look at discount online retailers like Martie.com, and check what they could around advertised pricing and offerings at local farmers’ markets.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Muse stuck to the basics like Ralphs, Target and Walmart, and then expanded to Sprouts, Vons and Martie.com when prompted.

After further nudging, Muse also reviewed user-reported pricing from Costco (I don’t have a membership), gave me vague information about Grocery Outlet, suggested Misfits Market/Imperfect Foods, ruled out Amazon Fresh and gave me some sample pricing from a local butcher for meats.

Muse also checked some local bakery prices after I mentioned I’d like to find alternatives to sweet treats at a more affordable price.

Dots checked all the usual places that Meta Muse checked with a few extras thrown in — Smart & Final for produce, meat and some packaged products, and Super King’s monthly ad.

Neither of the tools checked Trader Joe’s. Muse said that the grocery chain doesn’t show prices of its products online, despite me finding that the Trader Joe’s website shows prices of its products online. Both Dots and Muse admitted to only checking the local store’s pages when asked.

You could spend some time making a list from scratch of all the groceries you might need every two weeks and give that list to the AI agent for its search. You could also skip all that and take some pics of your most recent grocery receipts and online payments and have the AI agent build the list itself.

I had some receipts saved, and the AI agents I used for this test were able to put together an accurate grocery list from them. I also provided emails showing receipts from online orders of household items and groceries for the past month to build my orders.

Muse took my receipts and concluded that, surprise, surprise, shopping at Gelson’s was not as cost-effective as shopping at Walmart. It spat out a lot of specific information about comparisons on some of the items in my list, but completely ignored others.

Dots looked over my receipts and recommended I try shopping at Super King for most fresh meat, produce and a few grains, and keep Walmart as my main option for packaged products like pasta sauce.

Both tools tackled this task in a way I was not expecting — as a narrative exploration of my overall grocery spending, with generalized suggestions supported by specific examples of spot pricing.

It was kind of like they’ve been optimized to write essays about grocery shopping as a school assignment, and not to be agentic personal assistants for real-world work.

Maybe it was the way I phrased the task, but I was hoping to get an actual shopping list with price comparisons for each item, or at least equivalent items, and a guide on where and how to procure said items.

Neither AI agent provided this autonomously and had to be directly prompted to provide grocery lists in response to a very specific request.

AI agents are supposed to be pros at anticipating the needs of their human bosses. I was keen to see how the always-on AI agents would handle managing the recurring needs of a small household and if they might make any autonomous suggestions meant to save me money in ways I hadn’t anticipated.

At no point did Muse seem to be actually thinking outside the strict parameters of the review of the items on the receipts I sent. This felt more like a conversation about what I’d previously purchased with a side serving of attempts to get me to connect Instacart to the tool.

It was also the most confusing grocery shopping experience I have ever had. That might be on me, but I would assume the agent might ask some questions, like how often I usually shop, what my monthly budget for groceries is, and how many people and animals live in the house. Muse didn’t ask any of those things and had to be prompted consistently.

Muse sent me the final grocery list as a raw Markdown file, which was hilarious. I got a .pdf version after explicitly asking for one.

My original total with one in-person shop and one online order was about $180. Muse’s total with three in-person shops and one online order came in at around $153.

Dots was slightly better suited to this task after some prodding, and gave me some options for building prep meals with low-effort, high-protein recipes based on how I usually shop.

Dots’ final grocery list included everything I asked for, but still missed some key items, like solid substitutes for pricey prepped foods. Dot’s final total with two in-person shopping trips and a whole lot of missing items came out to around $85.

This test reinforced my findings about how agentic AI tools tackle domestic or traditionally invisible labor tasks. In my limited use cases so far, they are about as useful as a loose dog turd tumbling around in a clothes dryer.

If you’re not looking to book a flight and hotel for a business conference in Philadelphia or put your Sweetgreen order on autopilot, the AI agents exhibit a startling lack of curiosity and autonomous thinking.

There’s still a large gap in common sense on the part of agentic AI for those of us hoping these tools will make our everyday lives outside of a 9-to-5 easier, more affordable or less hectic. I hope that changes as they learn.

#3057. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is back after a stunning reversal

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Nvidia's board unanimously reinstated Huang 11 days after his removal, but his troubles may not be over.

Nvidia announced on Monday, Nov. 23, that it had reinstated Jensen Huang as president, CEO, and board member “effective immediately,” just 11 days after his departure. The decision comes despite ongoing investigations into Nvidia.

Also: Can AI plan terrorist attacks? The move is a quick 180. After Huang was removed as CEO on Nov 12 in response to numerous affairs with employees and a possible connection to a foreign intelligence official, Nvidia’s board said during last week’s earnings call that it would not reinstate him.

The SEC investigation is separate from national security concerns surrounding Alice Huang, the suspected foreign intelligence operative Jensen Huang admitted having an extramarital affair with.

#3056. South Korea proceeds with case over allegedly misleading Apple Intelligence ads

South Korea’s Fair Trade Commission (KFTC) announced today that it has opened formal proceedings against Apple over its advertising of Apple Intelligence features for the iPhone 16 lineup. Here are the details.

Back in March 2025, shortly after Apple announced it was delaying the rollout of what would eventually become Siri AI, the South Korean civic group Seoul YMCA filed a complaint with the KFTC.

At the time, Yonhap reported that the organization had previously called on Apple to compensate affected customers, and alleged that the company may have continued to market the iPhone 16 lineup despite already knowing that some of its advertised AI features would be delayed.

As 9to5Mac readers will likely remember, Apple widely advertised Apple Intelligence and AI-powered Siri features as part of its iPhone 16 launch campaign, going so far as to give its iconic Fifth Avenue store an Apple Intelligence-themed makeover and run an ad campaign starring Bella Ramsey.

This landed Apple in legal and regulatory trouble in multiple markets, including the US, where the company agreed to a $250 million class action settlement earlier this year, and Brazil, where it came under scrutiny from a consumer protection agency in May. Now, the company is facing potential penalties in South Korea as well.

As a result of Seoul YMCA’s complaint, the KFTC conducted an investigation into Apple’s advertising practices. Today, the regulator announced (via MacRumors) that its investigators had submitted their findings and recommendations for formal deliberation by the Commission.

According to the KFTC’s press release, investigators concluded that Apple advertised Apple Intelligence and its planned staggered rollout on the iPhone 16 website, but “concealed, downplayed, or omitted information material to consumers’ purchasing decisions.”

That included, per the press release (translation ours):

The press release also notes that Genmoji, Image Playground, “Siri with the ability to understand personal context,” and “Siri that provides step-by-step guidance” were not made available as advertised:

For example, Siri’s ability to understand personal context was advertised as being scheduled to launch first in US English by the end of 2024, as currently available in US English in October 2024, and as scheduled to support Korean in early April 2025.

In reality, the feature was not launched in US English until September 2026, with Korean language support following in October 2026.

As a result, the investigators concluded that Apple’s conduct constituted a violation of the country’s Labeling and Advertising Act, which prohibits deceptive labeling and advertising.

They recommended issuing corrective orders, including a prohibition on such conduct in the future, as well as imposing a financial penalty.

They also estimate that Apple’s revenue associated with the alleged violation amounts to approximately 3.35 trillion South Korean won (roughly $2.5 billion), though the KFTC’s press release does not specify the amount of the fine.

⁠Yonhap noted today that the fine could reach roughly $48 million, while ⁠KBS reported that it could range between $21 million and $42 million.

With the report now submitted, Apple has six weeks to submit its written defense before the Commission deliberates and makes a final determination on whether the company violated the law and, if so, how much it should be fined.

In a statement to MacRumors, Apple said it had cooperated with the KFTC’s investigation, but disagreed with the conclusions. The company also highlighted the rollout of Korean-language support for Apple Intelligence last year and, more recently, for Siri AI.

#3053. Pixel Weather’s new feature lets you report your local conditions now

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > Android

Weather apps are notoriously inaccurate. Google's new feature can help.

Default weather apps are notoriously unreliable, but Google is giving users a chance to help improve things.

A recent update to the Pixel Weather app introduced a new “Report current weather” card. If the weather you’re experiencing doesn’t match what the app is saying, you can report the discrepancy.

“Share the current weather condition,” the card says, “to improve local forecasts.” If you tap it, you’re given options to indicate whether the current temperature is accurate, warmer, or colder than the app says, and what your current conditions are, with options like cloudy, clear, rain showers, strong thunderstorms, and rain showers.

The card also shows what the app currently thinks is happening for you to use as the basis for your report. To find the card, scroll all the way to the bottom of your Pixel Weather app.

Also: Your best Google weather app alternatives (and what I recommend instead)

Google said that it will use your feedback, device location, and “some diagnostic information” to help improve forecasts. Nothing you report will be associated with your Google account. The app doesn’t explain exactly how Google uses your reports, but I’ve reached out to see if the company can offer any details.

As someone who doesn’t put a ton of faith into default weather apps, I see this as a step in the right direction toward regaining some trust. Even if it’s not yet clear how Google will use the information you send, the goal of improving forecasts is an important one.

Also: I switched to MyRadar as my main Android Auto weather app and don’t regret it

A large part of what makes weather apps unreliable is that they lack human insight, and a computer usually can’t grasp the nuance of a local forecast the way a meteorologist would. Experts have also warned of reduced weather forecast accuracy due to recent NOAA budget cuts.

The Apple Weather app has a similar feature, so it’s good to see Google catch up. I couldn’t find a feature like this for Samsung or any other native app. One of the most popular third-party apps, Weather Underground, does feature a “Report your local weather” prompt. It also gathers information from hundreds of thousands of connected home weather stations.

This new Pixel Weather feature was present on three Pixel phones in my home, so the rollout appears to be widespread.

Cypherpunk's 25% hash rate target and $56.5 million equity raise show how it plans to fund expansion without selling mined tokens.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 26, 2026 at 3:22PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Cypherpunk Technologies ›

Management launched its mining operation in August 2026, generating $9.9 million in revenue over its first 44 days. By deploying machines directly, the company produces tokens below market spot prices, adding to its treasury.

Cypherpunk accumulates ZEC tokens through both active mining and open-market purchases. The business raised its holdings to about 2% of total circulating supply, keeping capital allocated toward long-term network exposure.

Management signed a letter of intent (LOI), a preliminary agreement, to purchase up to $30 million in next-generation hardware from Bitmain. The commitment supports the long-term target of capturing 25% of total network computing power.

To fund these initiatives, management raised $56.5 million through an at-the-market (ATM) equity program, an arrangement that lets a company sell shares directly into the public market over time. This structure provides capital for operating expenses so the company does not have to sell the ZEC it mines.

#3051. You won’t see a Galaxy Watch 10 until 2028 – here’s why

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

In an effort to cut costs, Samsung is postponing the launch of the Galaxy Watch 10, according to reports. Here's what to know.

You might have to wait over a year to get the Samsung Galaxy Watch 10: Samsung will not release any new Galaxy smartwatch in 2027, South Korea’s ET News reported on Sunday.

In an effort to cut costs after two quarters of consecutive losses and amid global RAM shortages, Samsung Electronics’ Mobile Business division will switch from an annual release schedule to a biannual one for its smartwatches, according to ET News. The switch follows other cost-cutting measures that will reduce production of the tech giant’s midrange smartphone lineup while increasing its premium Galaxy S27 offerings.

I am not mad at this decision by Samsung. Annual smartwatch updates are usually iterative anyway. Take Apple, for example. Its Apple Watch Series 10, released in 2024, had the same chip as the Apple Watch Series 11, released a year later. The most recent Apple Watch, the Apple Watch Series 12, has the same battery life as the Apple Watch Series 11. I’d expect Apple to keep its new sensors, which debuted on the Series 12 and Ultra 4, for its Series 13 or Ultra 5 next year.

Samsung’s Galaxy Watch 9, which debuted with a different chip and added a few more hours of battery, had the same bones as the Galaxy Watch 8.

If some of the biggest features on these smartwatches remain the same for a few years, why not change the release schedule to a biannual one?

Also: Galaxy Watch 9 vs. Galaxy Watch 8: I’ve compared both models, here’s my advice

Some of the most substantial updates that arrive on new devices like the Galaxy Watch 9 or Apple Watch Series 12 normally arrive through software updates, which end up landing on previous watch generations upon launch as well.

By switching from an annual release cycle to a biannual one, Samsung could drum up more excitement and anticipation between launches, and be able to surprise its customers with more purchase-worthy updates.

Samsung did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Some at-home essential tips in case of a power cut

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Don’t be stuck in the dark without heat because you failed to prepare for a winter power outage. Taking a little time now to gather the right supplies will ensure you won’t be scrambling in the dark when the power goes out.

Heavy snow and freezing rain don’t just disrupt your plans — they can impact the grid. So, before the temperature starts to drop, gather the essentials you’ll need to stay warm and safe in an emergency. I've created the checklist below of five things to be aware of when it comes to surviving potential winter power outages.

I have a ‘battery drawer’ in my home that’s also filled with other random items, but it’s worth checking that it’s well stocked. Are those rechargeable batteries fully charged, and do you have a mix of sizes, with enough to charge the appliances you’ll rely on?

Sort through and focus on checking that you have batteries to charge any flashlights, radios and other essential items you will need during an outage.

Don't get caught stumbling around in the dark without a flashlight. Store one in an easily accessible spot — like a dedicated kitchen drawer — and make sure everyone in the household knows where it is. Better yet, keep a few extras on hand so there’s enough to go around, and double-check that they all have fresh batteries before a storm hits.

I’ve recently invested in an emergency wind-up radio in preparation for a power outage. It will keep me connected long after my batteries go flat. Apart from gaining access to emergency radio broadcasts, the product I bought has an added flashlight, an LED reading light and an SOS alarm. It’s worth looking out for these extra features, as they effectively turn one compact device into a multi-purpose survival tool.

This emergency radio is designed for power outages and other emergencies. It can be manually charged by the hand crank or the solar panel, and it also has a compartment for AAA batteries. In addition to tuning into emergency radio broadcasts, it features a torch, a reading light and an emergency alarm.

As soon as the temperature drops, I love snuggling up in the evening with a heated blanket — but that won’t do me much good when the power goes out. To keep the bitter chill at bay during an outage, I’ve stocked up on heavy-duty blankets that are built for real warmth, not just decoration.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

For something similar to what I’ve purchased, you could try this Olive Green Warm Blanket from Ever Ready First Aid for $27 on Amazon, it’s 80% wool and could double up as a camping blanket.

Between my induction cooktop, electric oven, and daily air fryer habit, I’d be in big trouble if the power went out. And when the temperature drops, hot food and warm drinks are essential for keeping my core temperature up.

Even if I’m keeping my meals simple and heating food straight from a tin — I certainly won’t be scrolling through recipes on my phone — I still want a reliable way to warm everything through.

That’s why I invested in a portable gas stove. I’ve kept things compact with a single-burner model. I like this Coleman 1-Burner Butane Stove, for $39 at Amazon. It fits a 10-inch pan and will last up to 1.25 hours on one 8.8 oz butane gas cylinder. Though if you have storage space and want something more elaborate than tinned soup, double-burner versions are widely available.

Best of all, it will come in handy as an extra burner when hosting for the holidays, ensuring my guests never have to pour cold gravy over their turkey.

You would have paid $900 for this Legion Go S configuration at launch, and as much as $1,608 after that as gaming component costs rise. At $850, you’ll be paying $50 less than the launch price.

If the Legion Go S isn’t the right handheld for you, you can pick from a few alternatives from CNET’s list of the best handheld gaming consoles.

The Nintendo Switch 2 is our pick for the best overall gaming handheld, with a CNET score of 8.5 out of 10. The Switch 2 has a slim design with a large 1080p display, and the controllers attach magnetically. We got less battery life from the Switch 2 than from the original Switch in our testing. Nintendo only lets you play games bought on game cards or through the eShop, so you won’t be able to bring your Steam library to the Switch 2.

Valve’s Steam Deck OLED uses the same SteamOS software as the Legion Go S. The Steam Deck OLED has an HDR OLED display, and we liked the controls and the improved haptics in our testing. Valve is selling the Steam Deck OLED for $789, which is $61 less than the Legion Go S. The Steam Deck OLED has a smaller screen than the Legion Go S, though, so you’ll have to decide if the $61 saving makes up for the difference in size.

The ROG Xbox Ally X runs Windows, so you can install games from multiple game stores without any workarounds. The grips are larger than the ones on most handhelds, which can make the ROG Xbox Ally X more comfortable to hold. We found the performance to be excellent in lighter games, but Windows can sometimes slow the handheld down. At $1,000 at Best Buy, the ROG Xbox Ally X is $150 more expensive than the Legion Go S.

It depends on the handheld. Lenovo has cut the Legion Go S with SteamOS to $850, from $1,608, for Black Friday. But we haven’t found Black Friday discounts on the Nintendo Switch 2, the Steam Deck OLED or the Asus ROG Xbox Ally X yet. You’ll find more discounts in other categories right now, including Best Buy’s Black Friday OLED TV deals and our Black Friday headphone deals.

Best Buy’s Black Friday sale recently had the same model for $901, which is $51 more than Lenovo’s price. Lenovo is also offering a price match guarantee and extended returns through Jan. 27, in case you find a lower price during Amazon’s Black Friday sale or Walmart’s Black Friday sale.

CNET Senior Editor Lori Grunin prefers the Legion Go S with SteamOS, which has a larger display and a different control layout. The Steam Deck OLED is still our pick for the best PC handheld on our list of the best handheld gaming consoles, and it’s the one I would buy myself. The Steam Deck OLED has an HDR OLED display, while the Legion Go S has an LCD screen. The Legion Go S and the Steam Deck OLED both use SteamOS, so you should be able to play most of the same Steam games on either handheld.

#3046. Claude can ship code for you now – how to try it (if you dare)

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Would you let a few Claude agents review and ship your code for you?

Developers are already using Claude Code and other AI-powered coding tools like OpenAI’s Codex in droves, but some still want to check code themselves before pushing it. A new feature from Anthropic is banking on more adventurous, agent-first coders being comfortable with handing even that last step off to AI.

With Claude Ship, announced in beta Monday, “Claude can now review pull requests, fix them, and approve the ones that a team’s policy allows,” Anthropic said. The feature is geared toward individual developers or companies who want to move faster or already delegate a significant amount of coding tasks to agents.

Also: How I made a paid Mac app in 11 days with Claude – without writing a single line of code

“Teams stay in control of where it runs and which changes need a person,” Anthropic said. “The goal: ship code as fast as teams write it.”

The new feature uses two new agents alongside Code Review, which Anthropic released in March for Teams and Enterprise plans. Code Review first collects, organizes, and posts findings to a PR. Then, Anthropic said, the agents, PR Steward and Claude Approvals, guide a PR “through review, fixes, and an approval decision so teams don’t have to manage each step manually.”

PR Steward can’t approve or merge pull requests, designed instead to participate “on failing CI checks, merge conflicts, and review feedback from Code Review, other review tools, or team members.” It also documents what it changed in each push through comments and provides instructions for how to undo it, should developers want to. Throughout the process, developers can give Claude instructions or tell it to stop by commenting @claude.

Also: I tried replacing Claude Code with a local AI stack that’s completely free – how it went

As a final step, Claude Approvals references whatever policy the user or team abides by (which developers provide as a repo file), and additional signals developers set, like Code Review findings, before approving changes autonomously if allowed. If not, it will seek out “deeper human sign-off,” Anthropic said.

“It works like a smarter CODEOWNERS file,” the release added. Anthropic said it recommends that most customers enable those additional checks “first in observe-only mode to build confidence before configuring branch protection to require Claude Approvals’ green light.”

Admins can select which repos the feature runs in and adjust settings as needed.

But Claude Ship isn’t meant for everyone.

“Most teams want a person to approve every change, and that’s easy to keep up with when people write every line. But a lot of developers build with agents now,” Anthropic said in the release, noting that its own Claude Code team uses Claude to write a majority of the code it needs.

ZDNET senior contributing editor David Gewirtz said the idea of shipping code at the speed of writing it “raises the hair on the back of my neck.” But he added that, given how impressive Claude Code has been thus far, he wouldn’t be surprised if Anthropic’s deployment tools were equally effective.

Also: Claude Code vs. Codex: Why I use both and how to choose one (if you must)

“But, it does depend on the team’s workflow and the kind of development they’re doing,” he said. “It’s not something for every team and certainly not for every indy developer, but for certain deployment processes, it could shave a ton of time off turnaround, saving money and potentially sending fixes to customers much faster. ‘Tis cool.”

Ultimately, Gewirtz said he has reasons to both trust the tool and be concerned about what it means for younger developers in the workplace.

“Since that workflow is often assigned to less experienced members of the team, it’s likely that Claude would be as accurate, although it’s one more way to shut the junior members out of where they add value to the company and grow their skills to become senior decision makers,” he said.

Developer platforms overall are adapting to the new state of the industry. Entire, a GitHub competitor started by the Microsoft-owned platform’s former CEO, is built around the expectation that developers are working side-by-side with agents.

Individuals with a Pro, Max, or Ultra subscription can use the /ship command now available in Claude Code, meaning “users won’t notice any significant increase in token usage,” an Anthropic representative explained. Team and Enterprise users can use Claude Ship for Github repos, and it is billed on tokens, so your overall bill may change.

Still, that depends on the number of PRs users are putting through Claude Ship — to avoid any surprises, admins can set spending limits for both Code Review and the new PR Steward agent. They can also turn Claude Ship on for an entire repo, meaning developers won’t need the /ship command.

Anthropic added that certain Enterprise organizations “can claim at least $10,000 in Claude Ship credits to use within 30 days of claiming” by Jan. 15, 2027.

Also: How I safely run my OpenClaw AI agent 24/7 on a Mac – with Claude Code standing guard

#3045. SpaceX Is a Great Company. Is It a Great Stock at This Price?

SpaceX's current stock price leaves no room for error.

By Prosper Junior Bakiny – Nov 26, 2026 at 3:36PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

Warren Buffett, perhaps the greatest investor of all time, once said: "It's far better to buy a wonderful company at a fair price than a fair company at a wonderful price." However, even a wonderful company isn't worth buying at just any price. Earning outstanding long-term returns requires investors to buy stocks at the right price. On that note, let's consider one popular company on the market right now: Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.30%). The company arguably boasts a very strong business, but is its current stock price fair?

SpaceX is a leader in rocket technology. It helped pioneer reusable rockets and became the leader in space launch, contracting a significant amount of business from various U.S. government agencies. The company has also become a leader in satellite-based connectivity. Its constellation of Low Earth Orbit (LEO) satellites is far larger than that of any competitor.

In addition, SpaceX offers various artificial intelligence (AI) services, including access to its Grok large language model and compute capacity through a network of data centers. It isn't just the breadth of SpaceX's business that makes it a great company. It has arguably built a robust competitive advantage. Consider the company's space launch business.

Building rockets is capital-intensive, but that's just step one. They then have to go through multiple stages of testing before they can fly regularly. That's not to mention the expensive and time-consuming process of obtaining the necessary regulatory approvals that allow companies to legally fly rockets. SpaceX has already gone through all that with its Falcon 9, a partially reusable vehicle that has helped significantly slash space launch costs.

It has also made tremendous progress with its next-gen rocket, Starship, which has now undergone more than a dozen flight tests. SpaceX's rocket technology is superior to that of its competitors. That's why it is the market leader by far. Given the financial and regulatory barriers to entry, not to mention the time it takes to develop and build rockets, the company has a strong competitive edge. There is something similar going on with SpaceX's connectivity business.

Launching satellites into orbit takes time, a lot of money, and the appropriate authorizations. Also, service quality generally improves as the number of satellites increases. That's why it won't be easy for competitors to catch up to SpaceX here. Further, the company's financial results have been improving. In the third quarter, SpaceX recorded $14.6 billion in revenue, up 161% year over year.

The company's Super Intelligence segment -- through which it offers various AI services -- posted $7.6 billion in revenue, up 830% year over year. Connectivity revenue was $5.2 billion, 69.5% higher than the year-ago period.

And space revenue was $1.8 billion, up 6% year over year. On the bottom line, SpaceX recorded a net income of $4.4 billion, versus a net loss of $1.1 billion in the prior-year quarter. SpaceX's improving financials, strong lead and competitive moat across two of its business segments, and fast-growing AI business make it a robust company.

Now for the second question: Is SpaceX attractive at its current price? Shares are trading at about $126 as of writing, and its market cap is $1.7 trillion. Based on its trailing 12-month revenue of $32.04 billion, the stock is trading at about 53x sales. That's very high, but the company deserves a premium, given the strength of the business and how fast it is growing.

Also, it's perhaps more telling to look at the forward-looking earnings multiple. SpaceX's current forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio, based on expected earnings over the next 12 months, is 44.2 -- that, again, looks high, and we have to factor in some uncertainty with the company's prospects. SpaceX is significantly exposed to regulatory risks across all three of its businesses.

Also, its fastest-growing segment, SI, which now accounts for more than half of its top line, is the unit where its moat is arguably less established. The SI business also risks a significant slowdown, which would reduce year-over-year revenue growth and margins, given how much SpaceX is spending on its AI build-out.

With all that said, I'd consider buying SpaceX at roughly 30x forward earnings -- that may undermine its prospects somewhat, but it would also give me a relatively healthy margin of error. But that means that at its current 44.2x forward earnings, the stock is too expensive, at least for me. I'd wait for a steeper decline to initiate a position.

#3044. The FCC Approved a Million SpaceX Satellites for Data Centers in Space

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

"I think this is a mistake," one astrophysicist tells CNET.

The Federal Communications Commission has approved SpaceX’s request to launch 1 million AI-equipped satellites to create vast data centers in space.

SpaceX initially filed an application in May asking for permission to launch the satellites, collectively known as Starmind. The FCC’s approval requires that SpaceX launch its first data center satellite by 2030 and meet the 1 million mark by 2040.

“It’s just really abrogating their responsibility to go, ‘Well, this is a project they’re proposing. Is this actually safe?’” Jonathan McDowell, an astrophysicist who tracks satellite launches, told CNET. “It’s a blank check. Launch as many satellites as you can, and we’ll just see what happens. I think this is a mistake on the FCC’s part.”

Orbital data centers have long been a goal of SpaceX CEO Elon Musk, becoming a pivotal part of his pitch to investors ahead of the company’s IPO in June.

“You’re power-constrained on Earth. Space has the advantage that it’s always sunny,” Musk said in March.

On its web page for Starmind, SpaceX touts its high-efficiency solar arrays on the planned satellites and the cooling benefits of space. It says that high-speed laser links will beam the AI results back to Earth through Starlink’s constellation.

Space experts I’ve talked to this year about Starmind have been skeptical. The technical challenges alone are dizzying; the scale of SpaceX’s ambitions is hard to swallow.

It’s taken seven years for Starlink to grow its constellation to 11,195 satellites, and that was already an unprecedented feat. Is it possible to get to 1 million satellites in 14 years?

“We do not have the launch capacity for any of this,” Ian Christensen, senior director at the Secure World Foundation, told me in a previous interview. “Not even within SpaceX to do their own constellation, let alone the other constellations planned.”

SpaceX has increasingly prioritized launching its own satellites instead of letting commercial customers hitch a ride on their rockets. Starlink’s share of SpaceX missions has gone from 54% of its Falcon 9 rocket’s missions in 2020 to about 79% in 2026, according to a Reuters analysis. One analysis estimates that SpaceX would have to launch 5,000 Starship rockets a year to meet its goal — a staggering increase from 330 rockets that launched last year in total.

Scientists have already been ringing alarm bells about overcrowded orbits as they exist now. The more satellites in space, the more difficult it is for operators to maneuver around each other. A single crash could turn into decades of orbital debris, which could cause a chain reaction that eventually makes space inoperable for everyone. And burning up all those satellites in the atmosphere when they reach the end of their lives has already started to leave ozone-depleting aluminum particles behind. A paper published this week in Earth and Space Chemistry estimated that SpaceX’s data center plan would multiply pollutants in the upper atmosphere by more than 150 times compared to last year.

“There are scientific papers out there raising issues,” McDowell said. “Yes, they’re unproven, but if I say, ‘Yeah, we’re not sure if this is going to destroy the atmosphere and kill us all, but it might,’ is the right reaction to go, ‘Well, you haven’t proven it, so we’re just going to ignore it’?”

The FCC’s approval came despite objections from Amazon, the environmental nonprofit Public Employees for Environmental Responsibility and the American Astronomical Society.

A representative for SpaceX did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

#3043. This hidden iOS 27 feature ensures you’ll never sleep through an alarm again

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Say goodbye to accidentally silencing your alarms.

A lesser-known iOS 27 feature includes a new alarm volume setting that gives alarms, timers, and ringtones independent volume control. You can adjust the volume settings for alarms and timers independently of your phone’s alerts and system sounds, so you don’t miss an alarm after lowering your ringtone volume.

Also: Apple reveals daily limits on your Siri AI use – here’s how much you’ll have to pay

There have been ways to adjust the iPhone alarm volume for a few years, but there’s an important distinction between what the new feature entails and what those methods involve.

Previously, you could adjust your Sleep Schedule alarms to always go off at a specific volume, regardless of the iPhone’s shared volume. Sleep Schedule alarms have a dedicated volume slider that you can set and adjust as needed, all configured within the Sleep focus. The new feature, however, lets you set an independent volume for all alarms and timers, not just those in the Sleep Schedule.

Alarms, Timers, Alerts, and System Sounds are set to match your ringtone volume by default, but you can turn them off in the Sounds and Haptics setting.

Also: 5 new Apple Health features you should try in iOS 27.3 – if your phone qualifies

Once you disable the option to match your alarm volume to your ringtone’s volume, you can set your volume for upcoming alarms. With this feature, you can maximize your alarm volume without affecting the volume of incoming calls and notifications.

#3042. Etihad’s New Credit Cards Are Primarily for Status Chasers

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The new cards offer perks like faster paths to status and elevated earning rates when purchasing tickets with partners.

Etihad Guest, the loyalty program of Abu Dhabi-based Etihad Airways, is expanding its U.S. presence by introducing two new credit cards. Most people won’t get much value from these cards, but they can make sense for Etihad frequent flyers who want elite status.

The two cards are the Etihad Guest Visa Signature® Card ($99 annual fee) and the Etihad Guest Premier Visa Signature® Card ($499 annual fee). Issued by the First National Bank of Omaha, both offer faster pathways to earning and maintaining elite status and the ability to earn extra miles on Etihad purchases.

They also offer extra miles on international transactions (neither has foreign transaction fees) and purchases with Etihad’s partner airlines, which include U.S. carriers like American Airlines and JetBlue Airways.

Here’s what you need to know about the cards.

The Etihad Guest Visa Signature® Card comes with a $99 annual fee, and it earns Etihad Guest miles at the following rates:

3 miles per $1 spent on Etihad purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on Etihad partner airline purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on non-U.S. purchases.

1 mile per $1 spent on all other U.S. purchases.

Welcome offer: Through Feb. 3, 2027, the card is offering the following welcome offer for new applicants: Earn up to 40,000 welcome Etihad Guest Miles, including 10,000 miles after the first purchase and an additional 30,000 miles after spending $3,000 within the first three billing cycles after account opening.

Elite status benefits: You’ll also get Etihad Silver status after your first Etihad purchase within 90 days of opening your account. Unlike most airlines, that entry-level status is enough to get you into the business class lounge when traveling through Etihad’s hub at Abu Dhabi’s Zayed International Airport.

You can retain that status by spending $20,000 a year on the card, or $3,000 a year on Etihad purchases.

The Etihad Guest Premier Visa Signature® Card comes with a $499 annual fee, and it earns Etihad Guest miles at the following rates:

5 miles per $1 spent on Etihad purchases.

3 miles per $1 spent on Etihad partner airline purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on non-U.S. purchases.

1 mile per $1 spent on all other U.S. purchases.

The only differences in rewards between the premium and standard cards are increased earning rates for eligible airline purchases.

Welcome offer: Through Feb. 3, 2027, the premium card offers a higher welcome offer compared to the standard card: Earn up to 60,000 welcome Etihad Guest Miles, including 20,000 miles after the first purchase and 40,000 miles after spending $7,000 within the first three billing cycles after account opening.

Elite status benefits: The premium card offers a faster track to elite status: automatic Silver status upon card approval and Gold status after your first Etihad purchase within 90 days of opening your account.

To maintain Gold status, you’ll need to spend $40,000 a year on the card, or $8,000 on Etihad purchases. Otherwise, you’ll have Silver status.

Wi-Fi benefit: After your first Etihad purchase, you’ll get 3,000 Etihad Guest miles, enough to cover Wi-Fi costs on a long-haul Etihad flight. You’ll then get those miles again after each cardholder anniversary.

Travel benefits: The card offers a statement credit for Global Entry or TSA PreCheck and select travel protections. If you already have a premium travel card, you might already have access to these perks.

The card also includes a Priority Pass lounge access membership. However, an Etihad spokesperson told NerdWallet that it’s the entry-level membership tier, so you’ll need to pay for yourself and guests each time you visit a Priority Pass lounge. Most premium credit cards in the U.S. that offer Priority Pass allow complimentary visits for at least the cardholder.

The Etihad cards make the most sense for Etihad’s frequent flyers who are flying to or connecting through Abu Dhabi several times a year.

That’s because the standard card’s accelerated pathway to Etihad Guest’s Silver status tier can unlock the ability to choose business class lounge access in Abu Dhabi as a status perk.

With the premium card, you get a fast track to Gold. If you select lounge access as a Gold status perk, you can not only bring a guest on the same flight into the lounge, but also access Etihad’s worldwide lounge network. That includes Etihad’s own lounges in New York and Washington, D.C., as well as most partner lounges at other Etihad destinations.

Bear in mind that while Etihad has many partnerships with other airlines, it’s not part of an alliance, so you don’t get lounge benefits when traveling on partners. You’ll need to be flying on an Etihad-operated flight to get into the lounge.

If you primarily fly with other carriers, then Etihad status will have limited upside for you.

However, the Priority Pass lounge membership that comes with the premium card does not have that restriction. You can access Priority Pass lounges while flying on any airline, although you’ll need to pay an additional per-person $35 fee each visit.

The earning rates on the Etihad cards are lackluster for everyday spenders. The bonus categories are for airline purchases with Etihad and its partners, as well as international spending.

That means your day-to-day purchases at home, like groceries, dining out and gas, earn only 1 mile per $1 spent.

Plus, there are more ways to earn Etihad Guest miles than spending on an Etihad credit card. For example, you can transfer points to Etihad Guest from Bilt Rewards, Capital One and Citi ThankYou.

Those programs offer cards that typically have better benefits and bonus categories, and they offer flexibility on how you can use your points and miles. With an Etihad card, you can only earn Etihad Guest miles, which expire after 18 months without qualifying flight activity. (If you’re not traveling on Etihad or its partners at least once every 18 months, then you definitely should not get the cards.)

One of the most interesting perks of the new cards is the bonus category for spending with Etihad partner airlines — 2 miles per $1 for the standard card and 3 miles per $1 for the premium version. Typically, airline credit cards reward customers only for spending on the airline, not partners.

There are better cards available, though. Some offer as many or even more points per $1 on flights purchased directly from airlines.

If you want the card solely to earn more Etihad Guest miles, then the answer for most people is no.

While you can earn more miles for purchases with Etihad and partner airlines, the earning rates aren’t attractive enough to justify the annual fees, especially when compared with existing cards with currencies that transfer to Etihad Guest.

Status chasers could benefit from the fast track to elite status — but you would need to be a fairly frequent Etihad flyer to really take advantage of your elite benefits.

You want a travel credit card that prioritizes what’s important to you. Here are some of the best travel credit cards of 2026:

No annual fee: Wells Fargo Autograph® Card

Flat-rate travel rewards: Capital One Venture Rewards Credit Card

Bonus travel rewards and high-end perks: Chase Sapphire Reserve®

Luxury perks: American Express Platinum Card®

Business travelers: Ink Business Preferred® Credit Card

#3040. New To-Do List Item: Find Replacement for Microsoft To Do

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

If you use the free app, set your task list for To Do's retirement next year.

If you, like me, are the type of person who makes a lot of to-do lists and can’t breathe a sigh of relief until you check items off them, I have some bad news. One of the best free options for organizing your work and home life is reaching the end of its life. Microsoft plans to retire its To Do app at the end of June 2027.

We’ve seen no announcement or acknowledgment in the app itself that To Do, which debuted in 2017, would be retired. But several Microsoft-watching websites this week spotted a blog post from the company recommending users switch to its Planner app.

Microsoft To Do is retiring. For work or school accounts, Microsoft Planner will be the destination for managing personal work tasks alongside assigned work. The updated My tasks experience begins rolling out in December 2026.

Microsoft To Do apps will retire on June 30, 2027. This includes To Do on the web, Windows, Mac, iOS, and Android, for both work or school accounts and personal Microsoft accounts.

The underlying To Do service, APIs, and related synchronization support will retire on separate, later timelines. We’ll communicate those dates and any required preparation separately.

But there’s a big catch for Microsoft customers who are on 365 Personal or 365 Family plans: Planner will not be available to them. That means Microsoft is telling To Do users on those accounts to find a comparable app elsewhere if they’re not on a business account that includes Planner.

The change was also mentioned in a separate Microsoft post about unified tasks and plans for Copilot and Microsoft 365 from Microsoft product manager Ashna Patel.

In a statement provided to CNET, Howard Crow, director of product, agents and AI at Microsoft, said the change consolidates task management across Microsoft 365. “We recognize this change will affect customers differently, particularly those using personal Microsoft accounts, and we’re committed to helping them prepare,” Crow said.

Crow said the company will share more information about the transition in the coming months.

While the end of June 2027 may sound like a very long time from now, the path Microsoft has offered for To Do users is not a great one if you want to take action now. There’s no built-in export feature in the app, though in the blog post, Microsoft promises, “Microsoft will provide a way to export your To Do data before the apps retire. We’ll update this article with instructions, supported data, requirements, and limitations when those details are finalized.”

Already, there are some pretty severe limitations even for those on Microsoft plans that support Planner: Existing To Do list items won’t work after that June deadline. “After that date, you will no longer be able to view or manage them in Planner,” the post said.

In the meantime, you can shop around for alternate apps, some free, including Google Keep, ToDoist and Notion.

To Do users, myself included, are not happy. As Windows Latest reported, comments in response to Microsoft’s decision included, “Do you actually hate your users?”

If this were simply a case of Microsoft creating software people didn’t like and then abandoning it, that would be one thing. But the To Do retirement is especially painful for those of us who were forced to move to Microsoft’s app a few years after it purchased Wunderlist.

Wunderlist was a German app that launched in 2011 and quickly became popular for its slick interface, easy-to-update task lists and its availability across web, desktop and mobile platforms. Microsoft absorbed some of Wunderlist into To Do and killed the original app in 2020 without ever offering all of the features that made Wunderlist great.

As someone who used Wunderlist for years and has been using To Do, I can tell you that while To Do has been essential in my work and personal life, it never became as intuitive to use as Wunderlist. It never handled predictive task creation as well as Wunderlist did (such as understanding as you typed the date of a task or if it was repeating). It lacked features for sharing lists or collaborating on them, and simply didn’t look as stylish. As far as aesthetics, the best thing Microsoft did with To Do was add a Dark Mode.

Wunderlist was a joy to use. To Do always felt like the knockoff diet version of it and never quite as good.

After several years, I simply gave up on the idea that Microsoft would improve the app or add any new features, but kept using it out of necessity given the lack of a built-in export option.

The death announcement is not surprising. Just sad.

RIP To Do, but really RIP to the last remnants of Wunderlist.
