#3014. FreightCar America: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Management is betting on an upcoming fleet replacement cycle, leaving open the question of how quickly volume returns to support the margin story.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than FreightCar America ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, FreightCar America (RAIL +4.39%) reaffirmed its full-year revenue guidance of $370 million to $410 million despite lower quarterly railcar deliveries. The business isn't relying solely on new railcar manufacturing. Expanding aftermarket sales and internal cost cuts could position the manufacturer for profit growth when railcar demand rebounds. Three things management said stand out.

FreightCar America acquired three aftermarket suppliers within a year, the latest after quarter-end. Organic parts growth and earlier deals boosted third-quarter aftermarket revenue by 65% year over year to $10.8 million with a 44.5% gross margin. This segment generates repeatable revenue that cushions cyclical drops in new railcar orders.

The company expanded its market share in addressable new railcar orders over the trailing 12 months while protecting its pricing discipline. This commercial momentum lifted total backlog value by 28% year over year to $284.5 million.

Management completed a workforce realignment in the second quarter that delivers about $12 million in annualized structural cost savings. The savings reduce fixed overhead without cutting manufacturing capacity. This operational setup could create operating leverage for the anticipated industry replacement cycle.

Railcar storage levels are falling across North America as shippers bring railcars back into service. The manufacturer maintains flexible production lines, expands aftermarket distribution, and reduces structural overhead, aiming to capture margin gains as order volumes rise.

Commercial focus remains tied to pricing discipline and customer retention across major railroads and industrial shippers rather than chasing low-margin volume.

#3013. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#3011. How to Get Grants for College

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Grants don’t have to be repaid, but the money can only go toward school-related costs. Here’s how to use grants to pay for school and how to make them go further.

Grants are one of the best ways you can pay for your education. It’s free money you aren’t expected to pay back.

Grants can be used for tuition, course materials and other cost-of-attendance expenses. Some grants, depending on who awards them, require an application or have requirements you must meet before you can get your money.

Here’s how to use a college grant and what it can pay for.

Federal grants come from the federal government.

State grants come from state programs and often can only be used at schools in that state.

Institutional grants come from your college or university, or even your program or major.

Private grants come from nonprofits, community organizations and individual donors.

To receive a federal grant, you must complete the Free Application for Federal Student Aid (FAFSA). Most states require the FAFSA for state grants, and a few require a separate state application. You’ll need to complete the FAFSA for each year you’re enrolled in school. You may need to meet certain eligibility requirements, such as income limits, to get some grants. The FAFSA is how you apply for the Pell Grant, which is a financial need-based grant. The FAFSA also determines eligibility for federal work-study, which is a job program rather than a grant.

Federal aid, including federal grants, typically requires students to meet satisfactory academic progress. This means you have to maintain a minimum grade point average and be on track to finish your degree on time, as determined by your school. Your school also sets how many credits you must take to qualify for grant funding.

Some grants — typically private or institutional grants — require an additional application that asks for transcripts, letters of recommendation and personal essays. Your school’s financial aid office or the grant provider will have details on applying for specific grants.

After you submit the FAFSA, you’ll receive a financial aid award letter from each school you applied to, which outlines how much in federal, state and institutional grants you’ve been offered. You must accept your aid to receive it.

Most grants will be disbursed directly to your college or university. Your school will apply the grant money first to tuition, fees and any other charges it bills, such as housing or a meal plan.

In some cases, you may receive more grant money than your school bills, which is the best-case scenario. If that happens to you, any leftover funds will likely be refunded to you. Refund timelines vary by school, so it may take some time to get your money. You can get your refund faster by setting up direct deposit with your school. You can then use the funds for other costs approved by your grant — some examples are detailed below.

If you have questions about your grant money, contact your college’s financial aid office.

Grants can help cover cost-of-attendance expenses. Generally, you can use grant money to pay for tuition, textbooks, class materials, transportation, housing and food. Depending on your school’s policies, you can use grant money to cover summer classes or study abroad programs, too.

Some grants come with specific terms about how you can spend them. Check your financial aid award letter or, for a private or institutional grant, the school’s or organization’s website to find specifics.

After your grant is applied to your tuition and school expenses, you can options to fill the gaps between what the grant covers and the total cost of your program. Some options include savings, scholarships, work-study, payment plans, federal student loans or, as a last resort, private loans. By using grants to pay for your college education, you may be able to borrow less.

To maximize the grants you’re eligible to receive, enter accurate information on the FAFSA and complete it as soon as you can — some funds are first-come, first-served. Also, don’t limit yourself to federal or state grants — search and apply for as many privately funded grants as you can find.

Your grant money usually goes straight to your college or university to pay your tuition and fees. Anything that is left over may be disbursed to you for other school-related costs.

Yes, but it depends on your grant. Housing is part of your cost of attendance, so you can use grant money to pay for off-campus rent.

You don’t have to pay back a grant as long as you continue to meet all requirements for the grant, such as staying enrolled. You can use grant money on school and school-related expenses. Some grants may require you to meet eligibility requirements such as a minimum GPA or state residency. If you receive more grant money than your cost of attendance for a given school term, ask your financial aid office if you can put that grant money toward next year’s cost of attendance.

If you have a federal grant like a Pell Grant, these are examples when you may have to repay all or part of the grant:

You received enough scholarship money to reduce your federal aid need.

It depends. You can usually use grant money to buy a laptop. Computers are considered course materials, which fall under cost-of-attendance expenses, so they can be covered by a grant. However, always double-check with the grant provider and your financial aid award letter to ensure that you’re following all restrictions. Whether you can use grant money to cover a laptop depends on your school’s policies and how much grant money you receive.

If you get more grant money than your school bills you, you’ll likely receive the leftover money to spend on other cost-of-attendance expenses. You can use this money on living costs such as transportation or housing. Grant money often goes mostly toward tuition, so a refund isn’t guaranteed.

Grant and scholarship money is generally tax-free when you use it for tuition, required fees, books and required class supplies. If you have additional questions, consider talking to a tax professional.

#3010. Prediction: These 3 Consumer Stocks Will Beat the S&P 500 Over the Next 10 Years

I think Ferrari, Hilton, and Amer Sports have the brands, the growth runways, and the discipline to outrun the S&P 500 over the next decade.

By Micah Zimmerman – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Beating the S&P 500 sounds simple until you remember how good the index already is, since it has compounded at about 9.9% a year on a total return basis going back to 1928. So a stock that beats the index over 10 years needs more than a good quarter, it needs a business that can keep growing over the long term.

When I look for that kind of staying power in the consumer world, I aim for three things: a brand that people pay up for, a long expansion runway, and a management team that protects both. Here are three consumer stocks that I think long-term investors should consider for the decade ahead.

Ferrari (RACE +2.96%) deliberately builds fewer cars than people want, and that scarcity is the heart of its economic moat, which is a durable advantage that keeps rivals from stealing its customers.

CEO Benedetto Vigna said in the third-quarter release that deliveries "reflect our clients' ever-deepening desire to make each Ferrari uniquely their own." Those personal touches add profit to every car without adding volume.

Demand isn't the problem. Vigna said on the call, "We continue to experience a healthy demand across all geographies, with an order book that enters 2028."

The risks are a premium valuation, at roughly 36 times earnings, and the shift to electric models like the Luce, which has to win over buyers who love engines.

Hilton (HLT +0.52%) mostly doesn't own its hotels; it collects fees by managing and licensing its brands to owners through a franchise model, which is an arrangement where a business pays to use another company's brand and systems. That lets it grow without spending heavily on buildings.

CEO Chris Nassetta said in the third-quarter release, "We achieved our highest quarter ever for organic openings." He also said on the call, "Globally, Hilton is still less than 6% of supply providing a long runway for continued growth."

Travel is cyclical, though. CFO Kevin Jacobs said, "RevPAR in China declined 5.4% in the quarter," referring to revenue per available room, which is a key hotel measure of how well rooms are filled and priced. Middle East conflict is weighing on that region, too, and the stock trades near 49 times earnings.

Amer Sports (AS +2.56%) owns Arc'teryx jackets, Salomon shoes, and Wilson tennis gear, and every segment grew by double digits in its latest quarter. CEO James Zheng said, "I am very confident in the future outlook for our company"

The U.S. opportunity is what excites me most. Arc'teryx CEO Stuart Haselden noted that the brand has about as many stores in the U.S. as in Canada, which he said "makes it very apparent the potential that we have to expand in the U.S." Management also said Europe's store traffic is a challenge, and higher freight costs are nibbling at margins.

None of these companies will beat the market every year, but I think long-term investors should consider owning all three and giving their growth stories the full decade they deserve. Great brands tend to compound quietly.

What ties these three together is pricing power, which is the ability to raise prices without losing customers, and I think that matters more over a decade than any single quarter's results.

Ferrari's buyers wait years for their cars, Hilton's owners keep signing up for its brands, and Arc'teryx shoppers happily pay premium prices for gear that lasts.

There will be rough stretches, whether it's a travel slowdown, a weak luxury market, or a cold winter for outdoor sales, but investors who hold through those dips give each business time to prove its case. In the end, patience is the edge most investors give up too easily.

#3008. Nvidia Is the Worst-Performing "Magnificent Seven" Stock This Year. Is It a Buy Before 2027?

Even with Jensen Huang back as CEO, Nvidia is drastically underperforming the S&P 500 and Nasdaq this year.

By Daniel Foelber – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) investors have been on a rather unenjoyable roller coaster in recent weeks. And every time the ride looks like it's nearing an end, there's another unexpected turn that unearths more questions than answers.

Founder and CEO for over 33 years, Jensen Huang, admitted on Nov. 8 to being unfaithful to his wife, then resigned on Nov. 12, only to be brought back as CEO less than two weeks later on Nov. 23.

That same day, President Trump was on a phone interview with CNBC and took credit for speaking with Nvidia's board and helping convince them to bring Huang back. Trump said, "I spoke to them. Let me just put it that way. And I convinced them. And I will tell you, it was a lot easier to convince them yesterday than it was a week and a half ago." Trump was referring to Nvidia's stock price and how it was easier to convince Nvidia to bring Huang back when the stock was cratering.

Nvidia has rallied substantially since Huang came back as CEO, but Huang and Nvidia still face an uphill climb of federal, state, and internal investigations linked to Nvidia misleading investors and potential national security violations regarding advanced Nvidia artificial intelligence (AI) chips leaking into China through backdoor channels.

The stock is still under $100 per share at the time of this writing and down 20% year to date -- making it the worst-performing "Magnificent Seven" stock in 2026. The Magnificent Seven consists of seven mega-cap tech-focused companies that include Nvidia, Apple (AAPL +0.43%), Microsoft (MSFT -1.25%), Alphabet (GOOG -2.02%) (GOOGL -2.13%), Amazon (AMZN -3.12%), Meta Platforms (META +3.40%), and Tesla (TSLA -1.18%).

Here's the simplest reason to buy Nvidia, and an argument for why it's best to keep the semiconductor stock on a watch list for now.

Heading into this year, some investors were concerned that Nvidia would be unable to maintain its breakneck revenue growth rate and high gross margins. But hyperscalers continued to ramp up AI capital expenditures. And Nvidia has broadened its customer base to other enterprises, AI start-ups, AI labs, and neoclouds.

As a result, Nvidia's earnings growth began outpacing its stock price growth, compressing its valuation. But then the unthinkable happened.

Nvidia's stock price has plummeted despite exceptional results and guidance for 75% revenue growth in fiscal 2028. Nvidia reported third-quarter fiscal 2027 results on Nov. 17, with revenue more than doubling from the third quarter of fiscal 2026. In the nine months ended Oct. 25, 2026, Nvidia generated $183.3 billion in net income compared to $77.11 billion for the nine months ended Oct. 26, 2025.

Nvidia is on track to surpass Alphabet as the world's most profitable company. It now trades at just 15.4 times forward earnings -- making it the cheapest Magnificent Seven stock despite having the highest gross margins and the fastest revenue growth rate.

For context, the forward P/E of the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.25%) is 17.3 -- meaning Nvidia is now priced like a value stock.

Buying high-quality companies on sale is an excellent way to compound wealth over time. Nvidia doesn't have to deliver blowout results to impress investors. In fact, its growth rate could fall substantially, and it would still be a steal at current levels.

The issue isn't Nvidia's valuation -- it's investors' concerns about where the company could be headed over the next three to five years.

Jensen Huang's sudden resignation exposed Nvidia's extreme vulnerability to key-person risk, governance failures, and an incapable board of directors. Going forward, Nvidia will need to change its structure to be less dependent on Huang, which could make it more rigid and less innovative but also protect long-term investors.

In addition to Nvidia-specific issues, there's also the risk that AI spending could decline considerably due to higher interest rates, which have made borrowing more expensive. With democrats taking control of the Senate and the House, there may be higher regulatory hurdles to accelerating data center construction. And there's the ongoing risk that compute demand will taper off as AI models become more efficient.

Granted, Nvidia is incredibly well positioned to endure a slowdown because of its industry-leading position and rock solid balance sheet. But it could also lead to stagnating or declining earnings in the medium term, which would challenge Nvidia's valuation.

All told, Nvidia simply has too many unknowns right now. Until investors get more clarity on the findings of the investigations, the company's succession plan for Huang, and the extent of its governance overhaul, Nvidia remains a hold.

Heading into the new year, I'd rather buy high-quality Magnificent Seven stocks with a clear runway for future earnings growth like Alphabet, even if its valuation is more expensive than Nvidia's.

#3005. Legend Biotech: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Manufacturing gains pushed gross margin to 58%, a key step toward the full-year profitability goal management is targeting.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:47PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Legend Biotech ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, Legend Biotech (LEGN -0.23%) announced that net trade sales for CARVYKTI, its cell therapy for multiple myeloma, a blood cancer, grew 36% year over year to $710 million. Adoption in earlier lines of cancer treatment and improved manufacturing efficiency are driving the business toward full-year profitability. Three things management said stand out.

Second- and third-line patients reached 50% of U.S. apheresis volume, the blood-cell collection process required to manufacture CARVYKTI, up from 41% earlier in the year. It's getting patients onto therapy at first relapse, which improves clinical outcomes and expands the addressable market before competing treatments take hold.

Gross margin, the portion of sales remaining after direct production expenses, reached 58% in the third quarter, driven by manufacturing gains. Operating income reached $46.8 million, which includes a $24 million one-time credit related to share-based compensation. It's on track for full-year profitability on an adjusted net income basis, a non-IFRS accounting metric that excludes non-recurring charges.

The U.S. Food and Drug Administration cleared the Investigational New Drug application, or IND, for LB2501, Legend Biotech's in vivo cell therapy that genetically modifies white blood cells directly inside a patient. It's seeing early clinical success, with a 100% overall and complete response rates at dose level 2

International expansion is accelerating across 20 markets, and updated treatment guidelines favor cell therapy before antibody treatments. Management is keeping a close eye on doctor referral patterns and manufacturing capacity as overseas sales scale up.

A deep cash cushion provides strategic flexibility as clinical trials evaluate earlier treatment settings and next-generation platforms. That balance sheet strength allows the business to scale production and fund research without taking on debt.

#3004. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#3003. Mortgage Rates Today, Wednesday, November 25: A Little Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Rising inflation could, counterintuitively, help push mortgage rates further down.

Mortgage rates are a little lower this morning, and where they'll go next isn't totally clear.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage ticked down to 7.05% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is eight basis points lower than yesterday and 33 basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Inflation data out this morning looks, at first glance, like bad news for mortgage rates. But depending on how markets react, we could actually see lower mortgage rates. Keep reading after the charts for all the details.

Below are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for perspective.

P.S. Markets will be closed tomorrow for Thanksgiving, and Black Friday's a half day, and mortgage rates don't move much when the markets aren't moving. I won't be here to talk to you about mortgage rates, either, 'cause NerdWallet gives us Thursday and Friday off. Happy Thanksgiving!

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Like I mentioned above, it's not totally obvious where mortgage rates will head next. Let's walk through the two scenarios.

Here's why rates could go up: This morning, we got October's Personal Consumption Expenditures price index, also known as PCE. It's the last measure of inflation to come out each month, but it's also the Federal Reserve's preferred metric, so PCE gets a lot of attention.

The stats came in mostly as predicted, though it's not like the predictions were good — inflation's been overly strong for a while. But the core year-over-year figure, which came in a tenth of a percent over predictions, could prove troublesome. Core PCE removes categories that can be extra volatile, so it's considered a measure that shows the underlying trend. In this case, the underlying trend is Not Good.

It's already pretty much a lock that we'll see a rate hike at the Federal Reserve's December meeting, because yes, raising the federal funds rate is the Fed's main tool for taming inflation. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but a rate-hiking cycle from the central bankers definitely keeps upward pressure on borrowing costs.

On the other hand, here's why that same inflation data could help rates go down: Over the past several days, we've watched the stock market go on a wild ride, with the Anthropic IPO and the return of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Yesterday, things weren't looking good for tech stocks, and these inflation numbers could make it even worse.

The amount that tech companies have borrowed to fund AI buildout is a significant concern for investors, since companies will have to cover those costs before investors see any profits. It's already a big task, and if everything costs more thanks to inflation, it's that much harder.

This AI-related anxiety is rough on the stock market, but it's been a boon for Treasury bonds. More demand for bonds pushes up bond prices, which brings down bond yields (basically, the return investors get on a bond). Mortgage rates for 30-year loans are generally benchmarked to the yield on the 10-year Treasury note, so here's why you care about all of this: Lower Treasury yields means lower mortgage rates, too. If investors keep stressing about inflation eating into tech profits, we could see mortgage rates continue to move lower.

That was a lot of info on inflation, but inflation's not the only major metric influencing rates.

Let's back up for a second. The Federal Reserve tries to keep the economy steady by focusing on two key goals, often referred to as the Fed's dual mandate. One side is price stability — keeping inflation reasonable. The other is maximum employment, meaning those who want to work are able to find jobs.

Next week we'll shift our focus to the employment side of the Federal Reserve's mandate, because the first week of the month is always all about employment data. On Tuesday, Dec. 1, we'll kick things off with the Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey (JOLTS). JOLTS can tip us off to trends in the labor market; stuff like whether folks are leaving jobs voluntarily or if companies have openings that are going unfilled.

On Wednesday, payroll firm ADP will release its monthly employment stats. ADP is looking at its own payroll data to get an idea of how the job market is growing or contracting. The numbers don't include the public sector, but ADP often provides a preview of how the jobs report will go.

The Employment Situation Summary, better known as the jobs report, drops on Friday. This is the big one, since it tells us the nation's unemployment rate. The jobs report's other major stat is, similar to ADP, how many jobs the economy added or lost in the previous month. Unlike ADP though, the jobs report is inclusive of everybody in the public and private sectors.

Lately the labor market's been looking super healthy, which is great news for the economy but less positive for mortgage rates. When hiring's strong, the Fed can feel safer raising the federal funds rate (that's the short-term borrowing rate the central bankers adjust). Higher rates tend to discourage businesses from borrowing and expanding, so if the job market's on shaky footing, the central bankers are going to be cautious about raising the benchmark rate.

What we're seeing now though, where inflation is clearly the problem and employment's just fine, is a recipe for higher rates. Again, the Fed doesn't control mortgage rates, but the central bank being in rate-hike mode puts upward pressure on mortgage rates.

If we were to see employment falter in this latest round of numbers, it still probably wouldn't change the Fed's plan. That would be one poor month for the labor market versus months upon months of inflation running rampant — so yeah, they'll keep an eye on jobs, but inflation's still the scourge.

Our most likely source of lower mortgage rates in the short term is what I described above; investors who are leery of inflation and the promise of AI looking to the safe harbor of the bond market.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you could start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.55% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3001. Not Nvidia. Not Micron. If I Could Buy and Hold Only 1 Artificial Intelligence (AI) Semiconductor Stock Through 2030, This Would Be It.

Taiwan Semiconductor offers a compelling way to capitalize on the artificial intelligence (AI) chip revolution without having to pick a winner among competing chip designers.

By Adam Spatacco – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:54PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing ›

For much of the last four years, semiconductor stocks have been some of the biggest beneficiaries of the artificial intelligence (AI) revolution. While Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) has become the king of AI accelerators, Micron Technology (MU -4.53%) is capitalizing on unprecedented demand for memory chips.

Although both companies have attractive long-term growth prospects, competition could complicate their investment narratives over the next several years. Moreover, as the AI infrastructure landscape evolves, identifying which chip architectures will dominate the market is becoming more challenging.

Fortunately, there's one semiconductor company that stands to benefit from virtually every major development across the AI chip landscape: Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (TSM -1.96%).

Between its graphics processing units (GPUs), networking equipment, and CUDA software ecosystem, Nvidia has an exceptionally durable competitive moat. Nevertheless, Advanced Micro Devices is investing aggressively in its Instinct accelerator portfolio, while some of Nvidia's largest customers are developing custom silicon.

Broadcom has emerged as an important partner in the market for application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs), most notably through its relationship with Alphabet and its tensor processing units (TPUs). Meanwhile, Microsoft and Amazon are also developing their own chip portfolios, including Maia, Trainium, and Inferentia.

Another potential source of competition is Space Exploration Technologies' collaboration with Intel on Terafab. Terafab is Elon Musk's semiconductor manufacturing initiative that could eventually reduce SpaceX's dependence on third-party chip suppliers. Although commercial-scale production remains uncertain, the project underscores a growing appetite for vertically integrated semiconductor solutions.

Micron faces a different set of challenges. While the proliferation of generative AI has ushered in a memory supercycle, memory-intensive applications such as humanoid robotics and autonomous vehicles should introduce additional tailwinds over the next several years.

However, Micron competes against Samsung and SK Hynix in the high bandwidth memory (HBM) market. Furthermore, memory pricing has historically been cyclical, leaving producers vulnerable to fluctuations in supply, demand, and profitability.

While Nvidia and Micron could both continue thriving, there is an alternative way to invest in the semiconductor industry without betting on a particular chip designer or memory manufacturer.

Unlike Nvidia and AMD, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (TSMC) primarily manufactures semiconductors designed by other companies. Its customer base includes businesses spanning GPUs, custom AI accelerators, smartphone processors, and automotive chips.

Against this backdrop, if Nvidia continues to dominate the AI accelerator market, TSMC benefits. Should AMD start to capture additional market share or custom ASICs become increasingly popular, TSMC still stands to benefit because it manufactures chips across these competing architectures as well.

Interestingly, Taiwan Semi also has exposure to the memory supercycle. While the company isn't a producer of conventional dynamic random-access memory (DRAM), its collaboration with SK Hynix involves manufacturing logic base dies for next-generation HBM4 solutions.

Moreover, TSMC's advanced packaging technologies -- particularly its chip-on-wafer-on-substrate (CoWoS) platform -- play a critical role in integrating AI processors with HBM. As a result, growing demand for more sophisticated memory configurations creates additional markets for TSMC beyond traditional wafer fabrication.

The most compelling aspect of TSMC's competitive positioning is the sheer magnitude of its market share. Industry estimates suggest that Taiwan Semi controls roughly 73% of the global pure-play foundry market. By contrast, the next closest competitor sits at just 6%.

Although TSMC isn't technically a monopoly, its dominance gives it the characteristics of one. The company's manufacturing expertise, technological edge, production scale, and customer relationships are simply too difficult for the competition to replicate at scale.

During the third quarter, Taiwan Semi generated $46.7 billion in revenue, representing 41% year-over-year growth. Meanwhile, the company's gross margin reached nearly 65%, and its operating margin expanded to about 59%.

Management expects revenue to reach $53.3 billion in the fourth quarter, implying 58% year-over-year growth. These results illustrate how TSMC's manufacturing scale is translating insatiable semiconductor demand into robust profitability.

Despite these impressive financial trends, Taiwan Semi stock trades at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 15.7. That valuation profile looks attractive for a business generating this level of growth and profitability while commanding such a dominant market position.

Rather than attempting to identify which chip designer or memory producer will emerge victorious over the next several years, investing in the company that manufactures many of the industry's most in-demand products looks like a compelling strategy.

TSMC's architecture-agnostic business model, durable competitive advantages, and reasonable valuation make it a compelling semiconductor stock to buy now and hold through the end of the decade.

#2996. CGI: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

#2995. Ready for ‘The Great Lock In’? Viral Money Trend Can Improve Focus

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Winter is the ideal time to make progress on your financial goals.

When it’s cold outside, it’s the ideal time to stay home, organize your finances, set some new money goals and make progress toward them.

That’s the promise of the viral winter money trend known as “The Great Lock In” that’s circulating on social media.

“You’re probably not doing too much,” says Robert Baird, wealth manager at Cornerstone Financial Services in Southfield, Michigan. “It gets dark early. So those can be great months to lock into saving and not spend in excess.”

One important component of the Great Lock In is developing new routines that will last well into 2027, says Jordan Mangaliman, founder and fiduciary wealth advisor at GoldLine Wealth Management in Fullerton, California.

“We want a long-term habit versus a Band-Aid fix,” he says. “In today’s economy, money goes in and out so blindingly fast.”

Ready to join the movement? Here’s how to get started.

What’s your money moonshot? Maybe it’s paying off all your credit card debt or purchasing a home. Or spending less than you earn. Defining it is the first step toward achieving it.

“Spend a lot of time identifying what that goal is,” Baird says. “Everyone’s in a different position, so comparing yourself to what you see online might not be applicable.”

Even while setting ambitious money goals, it’s worth grounding them in reality, Mangaliman says.

If you want to save a larger portion of your income, he suggests trying the new saving pattern for a couple of months to see how it feels rather than saying you’ll do it forever and then getting frustrated.

“It’s a marathon, not a sprint,” Mangaliman adds.

Once you’ve outlined those big goals for your Great Lock In, create a budget to help you achieve them, Baird says.

He suggests combing through your previous credit or debit card spending to see where your money is going. Then you can decide what you want to change or shift. Using a tool like the 50/30/20 budget calculator can also help. This approach allots 50% of take-home pay toward needs, 30% toward wants and 20% toward savings and debt payments beyond the minimum.

Baird suggests including saving as a line item in your budget so you don’t forget to do it. “Building that cost into your budget will put you in a better position later,” he says.

Any Great Lock In should include growing your savings, because that makes achieving other goals easier, Baird says.

“Maybe there’s something you could cut in the short term to build up that savings,” he says. Good targets include subscription services, food delivery, entertainment and travel costs.

Saving more is especially challenging in today’s environment, when everyday expenses like gas are higher than they were a year ago.

Baird says that makes winter a good time to try to reduce costs. There can be fewer temptations to go out and spend money when the days are shorter and the temperatures are colder.

Mangaliman recommends setting aside three to six months’ worth of expenses, although even a smaller amount is helpful. “Ultimately it’s about what you can afford,” he says. “It’s the foundation of your financial house.”

Along with building savings, paying down high-interest debt is another pillar of the Great Lock In.

With credit card interest rates higher than they were a few years ago, carrying credit card debt has gotten more expensive. “I would consider applying some savings to paying down debt, especially right now when your debt is costing you more than you can earn in savings,” Baird says. (You still want to reserve an emergency fund to cover unexpected expenses.)

“High-interest debt is one of the biggest thieves of wealth,” Mangaliman says. And one way to avoid turning to debt again in the future is to build up that emergency fund so it’s there for you if you need it.

Even when you are committed to a Great Lock In, some hiccups along the way are inevitable. Success depends on continuing anyway.

“It’s like a diet,” Baird says. “Just because you ate ice cream on a Sunday doesn’t mean on Monday you can’t go right back to being disciplined again.”

“Be kind to yourself and do everything in moderation,” Mangaliman suggests. Just wanting to improve your finances is already a great first step, he says.

In fact, he embraces the Great Lock In mentality himself. “There’s an old saying: It’s not how you start, but how you finish,” he says. “And you want to finish the year on a strong note so you carry those habits into 2027 and beyond.”

#2989. 2 Dividend Stocks to Buy in November and 1 to Leave on the Shelf

In a volatile market, you want stocks that have reliable businesses so you can sleep well at night.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 26, 2026 at 6:25PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Altria Group ›

Altria's (MO +1.95%) 5.9% yield is very attractive, but don't rush out and buy the stock. You may be better off with McCormick's (MKC +1.67%) 4.1%, or even Procter & Gamble's (PG +0.44%) 2.5%. Here's why two of these stocks could be buys in November, and one is likely best left on the shelf.

Altria isn't at any risk of going bankrupt. And its dividend doesn't look particularly risky, either. For more aggressive dividend investors, it may be worth a closer look given its well-above-market 5.9% yield. However, you have to ask yourself why that yield is so high before you buy the stock.

The answer is that Altria's combustible tobacco business, largely cigarettes, accounted for $5.4 billion of its $6.1 billion in revenues in the third quarter of 2026. That's 88% of the top line, which is a big problem when you consider that the number of cigarettes and cigars the company sold fell 2.9% in the quarter to 16.2 billion. In the third quarter of 2021, the company sold nearly 24.5 billion, indicating that this is a long-term trend. To be fair, Altria is working hard to diversify its business away from smokeable products, but given the uncertainty in the market today, thanks to geopolitical conflict, inflation, belt-tightening consumers, and AI volatility, why take on that kind of business risk?

At the other end of the risk spectrum is Procter & Gamble, one of the world's largest consumer-staples makers. The company sells paper products, toiletries, and makeup, with its brands typically positioned at the high end of the market. While there is some trade-down risk in that market position, P&G has long focused on leading the consumer staples sector with innovation. The goal is to produce products that consumers are willing to pay more for because they get more from them.

Right now, P&G's 2.5% yield is well above the market's 1% and the consumer staples average of 2%. So you aren't skimping on yield to own this well-run company. That said, its price-to-sales and price-to-earnings ratios are roughly in line with their five-year averages, so you aren't getting a bargain price, either. However, given that P&G was able to increase its organic sales growth by a respectable 4% in the first quarter of fiscal 2027 despite increasingly cost-conscious consumers, conservative dividend investors should probably give it a closer look.

McCormick's risk profile is a bit harder to define, but the risk-versus-reward balance is likely tilted toward reward. The historically high 4.1% yield is a key part of the equation, indicating the stock is cheap today. That's backed up by the P/S and P/E ratios being below their five-year averages. As with Altria, however, you have to ask why McCormick's yield is so high and its valuation so cheap.

The answer is that McCormick is in the middle of acquiring Unilever's (UL +2.48%) food business. That will roughly double the company's size and require it to take on significant debt. Those are risks, but the two companies' spice- and flavor-focused portfolios are complementary and offer cross-selling opportunities. And McCormick has a strong acquisition history, including meeting its post-merger deleveraging commitments. If you can handle a little uncertainty for a significant increase in yield, you may want to take a risk on McCormick as November draws to a close. Unlike Altria, whose business is contracting, McCormick's business looks set to get much bigger.

Altria's core business has been in decline for years. While the company has managed to maintain a growing dividend during this period, most long-term dividend investors are probably best off avoiding it. Conservative types would be better off with an industry stalwart like P&G. And if you are willing to take on a little more risk, McCormick's pending acquisition could be an opportunity to buy a historically well-run company while it is attractively priced and offers a well-above-market yield.

#2987. 4 Ways to Prep Your Finances Before the Next Tech Outage

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

You don’t need a basement bunker, but keeping some cash on hand might help.

If your social media feed is anything like mine, you’ve probably seen a reel or two on nonperishable food and solar-powered generators after the recent string of tech outages.

Have I considered giving water-filtering straws as stocking stuffers this year? Maybe.

But what about your money? If your area was hit by a natural disaster or your bank had an outage, are your finances ready?

Dan Stanush, senior financial planner at Charles Schwab in Asheville, North Carolina, recalls how fast financial conveniences disappeared after Hurricane Helene hit in 2024. “We lost power for three weeks and water for six weeks,” he said in an email. “And some local businesses could only accept cash or checks because card systems and ATMs were down.”

Don’t get caught unprepared. Here are the moves experts suggest to help you weather various types of emergencies:

Last week’s Chase outage was a good reminder that access to your bank balance can be disrupted. Stash at least enough cash at home — in small bills — to cover one week of expenses, says Jeff Judge, a certified financial planner (CFP) with Chesapeake Financial Planners in Forest Hill, Maryland.

If systems go down, ATMs can go down with them, said Eileen Olson, a CFP with Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina, in an email. Keeping cash in a safe at home is a good plan, but it’s also smart to carry cash when you travel. (Remember when planes were grounded nationwide a week and a half ago?)

“My parents and sister were traveling overseas on 9/11 and got stuck much longer than they expected,” Olson said. “After that, they always traveled with more cash.”

If your primary bank and its app were down, would you be able to pay bills? Having an account at more than one bank gives you an alternate funding source. Consider opening a high-yield savings account at a second bank or credit union for your emergency fund.

“This is the step I push hardest,” Judge says. “I had a client whose debit card, credit card and savings all ran through one bank, so when that bank’s system went down, she couldn’t even buy gas.”

If you don’t want to open a second bank account, a second credit card from a different bank and preferably on a different network (i.e. Visa vs. Mastercard) can provide another way to pay for things. “If there’s a problem with one credit card network, I have a backup with two other credit cards,” says Tony Steuer, author of “Get Ready! A Step-by-Step Planner for Maintaining Your Financial First Aid Kit.”

Make a printed list of your accounts, along with account numbers and key contact numbers. Include your bank, insurers, credit card companies and any financial professionals. Store the list with copies of essential paperwork, including insurance policies and their declarations pages, estate planning documents, legal certificates and identification — potentially in a fire-resistant file box.

Keep a digital copy of your documents in a password-protected, encrypted spot as well. A password manager can store all of your login info.

Joon Um, a CFP with Secure Tax & Accounting in Beverly Hills, California, suggests digital storage site Everplans to his clients, but there are a variety of sites that offer encrypted document storage.

Take some time to write down how you’d pay for groceries, gas and medication if your usual payment methods weren’t available. What expenses would you need to cover first? What recurring bills are on autopay? Where would you keep the cash and any key documents you’d want to take with you if you had to leave home? Make sure other household members know where everything is.

And set a reminder to update your records at regular intervals. Steuer, for instance, saves his account statements as PDFs and prints everything out once a year. “One of the big concerns is that people could conceivably break into your 401(k) and drain your 401(k) account,” he says. Account statements could help if a hack affects any financial balances.

It all adds up to a web of protection that (hopefully) you’ll never have to use.

“The goal is not to anticipate every scenario,” said Schwab's Dan Stanush, “but to have enough flexibility to meet basic needs while systems come back online.”

#2985. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2983. These 2 AI Chip Stocks Are Down 36% to 39% From Their Highs. Here’s What History Says Happens Next.

Nvidia and Broadcom shares have pulled back sharply even as AI demand holds up. Past sell-offs offer clues for long-term investors.

By Rick Orford – Nov 26, 2026 at 7:00PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -3.91%) and Broadcom (AVGO -0.07%) have both taken a beating, with their shares now trading roughly 36% and 39% below their 52-week highs, respectively. But history suggests that corrections don't necessarily mark the end of a bull cycle.

With both companies still benefiting from demand for artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure, could this sell-off present an opportunity for long-term investors?

Nvidia remains at the center of the AI boom, supplying the graphics processing units (GPUs), networking equipment, and software that power the world's largest data centers. That said, the recent weakness in its share price is notable, given that the company continues to deliver exceptional performance.

In its third-quarter fiscal 2027 results, Nvidia reported revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% year over year. Data center revenue alone reached $107.6 billion, up 110% from the same period a year earlier.

These results suggest that demand for AI compute infrastructure remains remarkably strong, even as investors become more cautious about how much tech companies are spending.

And while Nvidia stock trades around 36% below its 52-week high, it has eventually recovered to its previous highs each time. In fact, similar drops in 2020 and 2025 were erased within 56 and 82 days, respectively, of their lows.

Specifically, across eight recoveries from declines of at least 20%, Nvidia took a median of around 75 days from its trough to regain its previous high. But some recoveries took considerably longer, particularly following the late 2018 sell-off.

Of course, owning Nvidia stock comes with risks. For one, the company's recent leadership issues have introduced uncertainty, while U.S. export restrictions and competition from custom AI chips could limit some of its future growth.

Despite that, at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of just 10 times, Nvidia stock looks modestly priced for a company still growing revenue at triple-digit rates.

Meanwhile, the next company is focused on custom chips to drive long-term growth.

Broadcom is benefiting from a different part of the AI infrastructure opportunity: custom-designed chips and high-speed networking. This is where the company has established a strong competitive position.

In practice, Broadcom works with hyperscalers to design custom AI chips and supplies networking hardware to connect complex infrastructure for running AI workloads. Here's how that has shown up in the numbers.

In its third-quarter fiscal 2026 results, Broadcom reported revenue of $29.6 billion, up 86% year over year. More importantly, AI semiconductor revenue reached $16.7 billion, representing 221% growth. Management also projected AI semiconductor revenue of $21.7 billion for the following quarter, an increase of 236% from the previous year.

Right now, Broadcom's shares trade around 39% below their 52-week high. And like Nvidia, they've declined about as much in the past.

For example, in the 2022 bear market, Broadcom's shares declined by around 36%; by 48% during the COVID-19 crash; and by 40% during the 2025 tech sell-off. Yet the stock eventually recovered from those downturns. In fact, following its 2025 correction, Broadcom stock finished the year with a gain of about 49%.

There are still risks to owning the stock. For one, Broadcom faces competition from other custom chip designers, while a slowdown in hyperscaler spending could affect demand for its AI products. These are reflected in the stock's forward P/E of 17 times, which also looks modest given its strong semiconductor growth and solid free cash flow (FCF).

Nvidia and Broadcom are among the biggest companies in the AI infrastructure boom, but their recent pullbacks raise an important question: Is today's volatility a good buying opportunity?

Both companies have experienced painful corrections, but their underlying businesses continue to benefit from the ongoing AI build-out. Indeed, history shows that major drawdowns don't necessarily signal the end of a company's bull case. Ultimately, if earnings continue expanding and AI spending remains strong, today's lower share prices could look like attractive entry points.

As for what comes next, Nvidia could suit value-minded investors, given its lower forward P/E. Broadcom could appeal to those seeking faster AI semiconductor growth and greater upside to analyst targets.

#2977. 1 Incredibly Cheap Artificial Intelligence (AI) Stock to Buy Before It Doubles in 2027

The booming demand for AI servers is translating into phenomenal growth for Dell Technologies.

By Harsh Chauhan – Nov 26, 2026 at 7:08PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Dell Technologies ›

Though artificial intelligence (AI) stocks have taken a beating lately, the financial performance of companies in this sector remains solid.

We have already seen that several AI infrastructure companies, specifically semiconductor stocks, have delivered impressive growth in revenue and earnings this earnings season. And now, Dell Technologies (DELL +0.60%), another key component of the AI data center ecosystem, has delivered phenomenal results.

Let's take a closer look at Dell's performance and guidance and check why Wall Street is bullish about this tech stock's prospects over the coming year.

Dell released its fiscal 2027 third-quarter results (for the three months ended Oct. 30) on Nov. 24. The company's revenue jumped by 103% year over year to a record $54.9 billion, well above the $49.3 billion consensus estimate. Dell's non-GAAP earnings per share (EPS) increased at a significantly faster pace of 265% year over year to a record $9.45. Wall Street would have settled for EPS of $6.59.

The incredible demand for the company's AI-optimized servers is behind this stunning performance. The company sold $22 billion worth of AI-optimized servers last quarter, up by 291% from the prior-year period. What's more, even traditional servers and networking equipment were in terrific demand, with revenue from their sales increasing by 235% year over year to $15 billion.

What's worth noting is that Dell received way more orders than it fulfilled last quarter. Specifically, the company booked $60.2 billion in new orders for its AI servers, taking its total AI backlog to $132.3 billion. It is worth noting that Dell received $60.9 billion in AI server orders in fiscal Q2 as well, which helps explain why its revenue pipeline continues to improve.

Healthy demand for AI servers has prompted Dell to raise its annual guidance once again. The company now expects 78% revenue growth in fiscal 2027 to $202 billion, higher than the previous estimate of $192 billion. It was originally expecting a 23% increase in revenue at the beginning of the fiscal year, to $140 billion. Even better, Dell now sees its fiscal 2027 non-GAAP EPS jumping by 188% to $29.68, more than double its $12.90 EPS guidance at the beginning of the year.

Though Dell hasn't issued fiscal 2028 guidance yet, I won't be surprised if the company sustains its phenomenal momentum next year. That's because Dell already has a massive backlog, pointing to a significant increase in AI server revenue next year. The company is on track to end fiscal 2027 with $78 billion in AI revenue, more than 3x the $25 billion it generated from this segment last year.

Dell's backlog is likely to increase again this quarter, as the company notes that its order "pipeline grew sequentially and remains multiples of our backlog." That should set up Dell's AI server business for another year of exponential growth in fiscal 2028 (which ends in January 2028 and overlaps with most of calendar 2027).

A key reason why Dell has been witnessing such outstanding growth in AI servers is that its solutions are allowing customers to quickly deploy AI infrastructure into production. The company notes that it enjoys an advantage of four to six weeks over competitors during large-scale server deployments, which allows its "customers to convert backlog into revenue sooner and more reliably."

So, Dell stock seems well-placed for another impressive year in 2027 following 125% returns in 2026 as of this writing. That's exactly what Wall Street expects from the stock.

Dell has a 12-month median price target of $512.50, according to estimates compiled by CNN. That's 72% higher than its current stock price.

It is easy to see why analysts expect such strong upside from Dell next year. The company is growing at a red-hot pace and has a price-to-earnings ratio of just 12.5, lower than the S&P 500 index's earnings multiple of 20.3. The average earnings growth of S&P 500 companies is expected to be 33% in 2026, and Dell is on track to grow at a significantly faster pace.

Also, Dell's healthy backlog should help it outperform Wall Street's expectations again next year. It is worth noting that analysts have raised their earnings-per-share estimates for Dell following its latest report.

DELL EPS Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

Assuming Dell's EPS reaches $32.87 in fiscal 2028 (which ends in January 2028) and it trades at 20 times earnings, in line with the S&P 500's earnings multiple, the stock could jump to $657. That's 117% higher than the current stock price, suggesting that this AI stock could more than double over the next year. As such, buying Dell is a no-brainer right now considering its phenomenal growth and cheap valuation, which should set the stock up for a solid 2027.
